In the landmark Kesavananda Bharati case (1973), the Supreme Court ruled that the Preamble is an integral part of the Constitution and contains the "basic structure" of the document. Therefore, laws and constitutional amendments must align with the foundational values enshrined in the Preamble. It serves as a moral and political compass, guiding the legislature in enacting welfare laws and aiding the executive and judiciary in interpreting and applying those laws in a manner that upholds the spirit of the Constitution.

### 11 b) Explain different ways for acquiring Indian citizenship. (8 marks)
Citizenship in India is governed by Part II of the Constitution (Articles 5 to 11) and, more comprehensively, by the Citizenship Act of 1955. The Act prescribes five distinct ways through which a person can acquire Indian citizenship, reflecting a balance between bloodline descent and territorial belonging. 

1. **By Birth (Jus Soli):** Any person born in India on or after January 26, 1950, is generally considered a citizen by birth. However, due to amendments to curb illegal immigration, the rules have tightened. Currently, a person born in India on or after December 3, 2004, is considered a citizen only if both parents are citizens of India, or if one parent is a citizen and the other is not an illegal migrant at the time of birth.
2. **By Descent (Jus Sanguinis):** A person born outside India can acquire citizenship by descent if either of their parents is a citizen of India at the time of their birth. The birth must be registered at an Indian consulate within one year. 
3. **By Registration:** Persons of Indian origin who have been ordinarily resident in India for seven years, persons married to an Indian citizen who have been resident for seven years, and minor children of Indian citizens can apply for citizenship through registration. 
4. **By Naturalisation:** Foreigners (not illegal migrants) who have resided in India for a specified period (generally 12 years, though exceptions exist) can acquire citizenship by naturalisation. They must fulfill certain conditions, such as having a good character, adequate knowledge of an Eighth Schedule language, and intending to reside in India.
5. **By Incorporation of Territory:** If any new territory becomes part of India, the Government of India specifies by order the individuals of that territory who shall become citizens of India. For instance, when Pondicherry became a part of India, the Citizenship (Pondicherry) Order, 1962, was issued to grant citizenship.

### 12 a) Explain the salient features of Indian constitution. (8 marks)
The Constitution of India, adopted on November 26, 1949, and enacted on January 26, 1950, is a monumental legal document characterized by several salient features. 
Firstly, it is the **longest written constitution** in the world. Its sheer volume (originally 395 Articles, 22 Parts, 8 Schedules) arises from the need to govern a vast, diverse nation and includes detailed administrative provisions and special safeguards for minorities and tribal areas. 
Secondly, it is a unique **blend of rigidity and flexibility**. While basic features require a rigid amendment process (two-thirds majority in Parliament plus ratification by half the states), many provisions can be amended by a simple parliamentary majority, allowing the Constitution to adapt to changing times.
Thirdly, it establishes a **Federal System with Unitary Bias**. It divides power between the Centre and States but retains strong unitary features, such as a strong Centre, single citizenship, all-India services, and emergency provisions that can transform the federal structure into a unitary one during crises.
Fourthly, it provides a **Parliamentary Form of Government**, drawn from the British Westminster model, emphasizing the accountability of the executive to the legislature. The President is the nominal head, while real power rests with the Prime Minister and the Council of Ministers.
Fifthly, the inclusion of **Fundamental Rights (Part III)** guarantees civil liberties against state encroachment, protected by an independent judiciary. 
Complementing these are the **Directive Principles of State Policy (Part IV)**, which act as socio-economic goals for the government, and the **Fundamental Duties (Part IVA)**, which outline the moral obligations of citizens. 
Lastly, it established an **Independent and Integrated Judiciary**, with the Supreme Court at the apex, ensuring the uniform application of civil and criminal laws across the country and acting as the guardian of the Constitution.

### 12 b) Write notes on methods of termination of Indian citizenship. (6 marks)
The Citizenship Act of 1955 not only provides methods for acquiring citizenship but also outlines three specific ways by which an individual can lose their Indian citizenship: Renunciation, Termination, and Deprivation.

1. **Renunciation:** An adult Indian citizen of full age and capacity can voluntarily declare their intent to renounce their Indian citizenship. Upon the registration of this declaration by the prescribed authority, that person ceases to be an Indian citizen. Notably, when a male person renounces his citizenship, every minor child of his also ceases to be an Indian citizen. However, such a child can resume Indian citizenship by making a declaration within one year of attaining full age (18 years).
2. **Termination:** India strictly follows the principle of single citizenship. Therefore, if a citizen of India voluntarily acquires the citizenship of another country (by naturalization, registration, or any other voluntary means), their Indian citizenship automatically terminates. This provision prevents dual citizenship, which is generally not permitted under the Indian Constitution.
3. **Deprivation:** This is a compulsory termination of citizenship by the Central Government. It applies primarily to those who acquired citizenship through registration or naturalization (not by birth or descent). The government can deprive a person of their citizenship if it is satisfied that the citizenship was obtained by fraud, false representation, or concealment of material facts. It can also be invoked if the citizen has shown disloyalty to the Constitution, has unlawfully traded or communicated with an enemy during a war, or has been imprisoned in any country for two years within five years of acquiring Indian citizenship.

## Module - 2

### 13 a) Explain the concept of appeal by special leave. (6 marks)
The concept of Appeal by Special Leave is enshrined in Article 136 of the Constitution of India, providing the Supreme Court with an extraordinary and discretionary appellate jurisdiction. Unlike regular appeals, which are brought before the Supreme Court as a matter of right (under Articles 132, 133, and 134) when a High Court issues a certificate, Article 136 allows the Supreme Court to grant special leave to appeal against any judgment, decree, determination, sentence, or order in any cause or matter passed or made by any court or tribunal in the territory of India.

The primary objective of Article 136 is to ensure that justice is not thwarted by procedural technicalities or statutory limitations. The Supreme Court uses this power sparingly and only in exceptional circumstances where a substantial question of law of general public importance is involved, or where there has been a gross miscarriage of justice by a lower court or tribunal. It is important to note that this is a residual power; a party cannot claim special leave as a constitutional right. The Supreme Court has absolute discretion to grant or deny the petition. Furthermore, the provision specifically excludes military tribunals or court-martials from its purview. Through this mechanism, the Supreme Court functions as the ultimate arbiter of justice, stepping in to correct egregious errors regardless of the level of the court where the error originated.

### 13 b) Discuss the classification of Directive Principles of State Policy in detail. (8 marks)
The Directive Principles of State Policy (DPSP), contained in Part IV (Articles 36 to 51) of the Constitution, are non-justiciable guidelines meant to guide the State in policy-making and administration. Their ultimate aim is to establish a welfare state based on social and economic justice. While the Constitution does not formally classify them, scholars typically categorize the DPSPs into three broad groups based on their ideological content and objectives: Socialistic, Gandhian, and Liberal-Intellectual principles.

**1. Socialistic Principles:** These principles reflect the ideology of socialism and aim to lay down the framework of a democratic socialist state. They focus on providing social and economic justice, reducing inequalities, and ensuring an equitable distribution of resources. Key examples include Article 38 (promoting the welfare of the people by minimizing inequalities in income and status), Article 39 (securing the right to an adequate means of livelihood, equal pay for equal work, and preventing the concentration of wealth), Article 41 (right to work, education, and public assistance in cases of unemployment, old age, or sickness), and Article 43 (securing a living wage and a decent standard of life for workers).

**2. Gandhian Principles:** Based on the ideology of Mahatma Gandhi, these principles represent the program of reconstruction enunciated during the national movement. They focus on empowering rural India, promoting cottage industries, and uplifting marginalized communities. Important articles include Article 40 (organizing village panchayats and endowing them with powers of self-government), Article 43 (promoting cottage industries on an individual or co-operative basis in rural areas), Article 46 (promoting the educational and economic interests of Scheduled Castes, Scheduled Tribes, and other weaker sections), and Article 47 (prohibiting the consumption of intoxicating drinks and drugs that are injurious to health).

**3. Liberal-Intellectual Principles:** These principles reflect the ideology of liberalism and focus on modernity, internationalism, and progressive reforms. Prominent directives in this category include Article 44 (securing a Uniform Civil Code for all citizens throughout the country), Article 45 (providing early childhood care and education for all children until the age of six), Article 50 (separating the judiciary from the executive in the public services of the State), and Article 51 (promoting international peace and security, and maintaining just and honorable relations between nations). Together, these three categories form a comprehensive blueprint for India's socio-economic development.

### 14 a) What do you mean by right against exploitation? Explain. (7 marks)
The Right against Exploitation is a crucial fundamental right enshrined in Articles 23 and 24 of the Constitution of India, aimed at protecting human dignity and preventing the vulnerable from being subjected to abusive practices. In a society historically plagued by severe inequalities and systemic oppression, this right acts as a shield against physical and economic exploitation by both the State and private individuals.

**Article 23** prohibits "traffic in human beings and *begar* and other similar forms of forced labour." Traffic in human beings involves the buying and selling of men, women, and children like commodities, often for immoral purposes such as prostitution or slavery. *Begar* refers to the practice of forcing a person to work without remuneration. The article outlaws all forms of forced labor, ensuring that no individual can be compelled to work against their will, even if some remuneration is offered, as established in various Supreme Court rulings regarding debt bondage. However, an exception exists: the State can impose compulsory service for public purposes (like military conscription or social service) without discrimination based on religion, race, caste, or class.

**Article 24** specifically addresses child labor. It strictly prohibits the employment of children below the age of 14 years in any factory, mine, or other hazardous employment (such as construction work or railways). This provision is vital for protecting the health, safety, and childhood of young individuals, ensuring they have the opportunity for education and proper development rather than being forced into dangerous labor. Subsequent legislation, like the Child Labour (Prohibition and Regulation) Act, has further strengthened these constitutional mandates, creating a legal framework to eradicate exploitation.

### 14 b) Distinguish between fundamental rights and directive principles of state policy. (7 marks)
Fundamental Rights (Part III, Articles 12-35) and Directive Principles of State Policy (Part IV, Articles 36-51) are both foundational to the Indian Constitution, yet they differ significantly in their nature, scope, and enforceability. The distinction between them is essential for understanding the balance of power between the citizen and the State.

1. **Enforceability (Justiciability):** This is the most critical distinction. Fundamental Rights are justiciable, meaning a citizen can directly approach the courts (Supreme Court under Article 32 or High Court under Article 226) for their enforcement if they are violated by the State. In contrast, Directive Principles are non-justiciable. They are moral guidelines for the State, and a citizen cannot sue the government in a court of law for failing to implement a DPSP.
2. **Nature of Obligations:** Fundamental Rights are generally negative obligations on the State. They dictate what the State *cannot* do (e.g., the State cannot deny equality, cannot curb freedom of speech without reasonable restriction). They act as limitations on state power to protect individual liberty. Directive Principles, conversely, are positive obligations. They dictate what the State *should* do to promote social and economic welfare (e.g., providing a living wage, free legal aid, protecting the environment).
3. **Primary Objective:** Fundamental Rights aim at establishing political democracy. They protect civil liberties and prevent the establishment of authoritarian rule. Directive Principles aim at establishing social and economic democracy. They seek to create a welfare state by addressing issues of poverty, inequality, and social justice.
4. **Subordination in Conflict:** Historically, courts held that in case of a direct conflict, Fundamental Rights prevail over DPSPs. However, the current settled legal position (as per the Minerva Mills case, 1980) is that the Constitution rests on the "bedrock of balance" between the two. While Fundamental Rights are supreme, courts interpret them harmoniously with DPSPs to achieve the broader goals of the Constitution.

## Module - 3

### 15 a) Explain the powers of President of India. (8 marks)
The President of India, as the ceremonial head of state and the first citizen, is vested with extensive powers by the Constitution. Although the President generally acts on the aid and advice of the Council of Ministers (Article 74), these powers are vast and categorized into several domains:

**1. Executive Powers:** All executive actions of the Government of India are formally taken in the President's name. The President appoints the Prime Minister and, on their advice, the other Ministers. Crucial appointments, including the Attorney General, the Comptroller and Auditor General, Chief Election Commissioner, Chairman and members of the UPSC, and Governors of states, are made by the President. 
**2. Legislative Powers:** The President is an integral part of the Parliament. The President summons and prorogues the sessions of Parliament and can dissolve the Lok Sabha. A bill passed by Parliament becomes law only after receiving the President's assent (who can give assent, withhold it, or return it for reconsideration). Most importantly, under Article 123, the President can promulgate Ordinances when Parliament is not in session, which have the same force as an Act of Parliament.
**3. Judicial and Pardoning Powers:** The President appoints the Chief Justice and other judges of the Supreme Court and High Courts. Under Article 72, the President has the sovereign power to grant pardons, reprieves, respites, or remissions of punishment, or to suspend, remit or commute sentences in certain cases, notably court-martials and death sentences.
**4. Financial Powers:** Money bills can be introduced in Parliament only with the President's prior recommendation. The President causes the Annual Financial Statement (Budget) to be laid before Parliament and constitutes the Finance Commission every five years.
**5. Emergency Powers:** The President wields extraordinary powers during crises. They can declare a National Emergency (Article 352) due to war or armed rebellion, President's Rule in a state (Article 356) if the constitutional machinery fails, and a Financial Emergency (Article 360) if the country's financial stability is threatened. 

### 15 b) Explain the constitutional position and essential qualifications of Vice-president of India. (6 marks)
The Vice-President of India occupies the second-highest constitutional office in the country, modeled loosely on the American Vice-Presidency. The primary constitutional role of the Vice-President is to act as the *ex-officio* Chairman of the Rajya Sabha (Council of States), performing duties similar to those of the Speaker of the Lok Sabha—presiding over sessions, maintaining order, and deciding points of order.

The most critical constitutional position of the Vice-President arises during a vacancy in the office of the President. Under Article 65, if the President's office falls vacant due to death, resignation, impeachment, or other reasons, the Vice-President acts as the President until a new President is elected (which must happen within six months). Furthermore, if the President is temporarily unable to discharge their duties due to absence or illness, the Vice-President discharges those functions. It is important to note that when acting as President or discharging the President's functions, the Vice-President ceases to perform the duties of the Chairman of the Rajya Sabha and is entitled to the salary and privileges of the President.

**Qualifications:** To be eligible for election as Vice-President, Article 66 stipulates that a person must:
1. Be a citizen of India.
2. Have completed the age of thirty-five years.
3. Be qualified for election as a member of the Rajya Sabha (which implies they must possess qualifications prescribed by Parliament).
4. Not hold any office of profit under the Union government, any state government, or any local authority. (Sitting Presidents, Vice-Presidents, Governors, and Ministers are exempt from this condition).

### 16 a) Explain the qualification and disqualification for membership in the house of the people. (6 marks)
The House of the People, or the Lok Sabha, is the lower house of India's bicameral Parliament, representing the citizens directly. The Constitution and the Representation of the People Act, 1951, outline specific criteria for who can serve as a Member of Parliament (MP) in the Lok Sabha.

**Qualifications:** According to Article 84 of the Constitution, to be qualified for membership in the Lok Sabha, a person must:
1. Be a citizen of India.
2. Make and subscribe to an oath or affirmation bearing true faith and allegiance to the Constitution.
3. Be at least 25 years of age.
4. Possess other qualifications prescribed by Parliament. Under the Representation of the People Act, a candidate must be registered as an elector (voter) in any parliamentary constituency in India. To contest from a seat reserved for Scheduled Castes or Scheduled Tribes, they must belong to that specific category.

**Disqualifications:** Article 102 details the conditions under which a person is disqualified from being chosen as, or for being, an MP. These include:
1. Holding any office of profit under the Government of India or a State Government (exceptions apply for ministers and offices exempted by Parliament).
2. Being declared of unsound mind by a competent court.
3. Being an undischarged insolvent.
4. Ceasing to be a citizen of India, or voluntarily acquiring citizenship of a foreign state, or being under an acknowledgment of allegiance to a foreign state.
5. Furthermore, Parliament has enacted statutory disqualifications (e.g., conviction for certain crimes resulting in imprisonment of two years or more, corrupt electoral practices). Additionally, the Tenth Schedule (Anti-Defection Law) disqualifies members if they voluntarily give up membership of their political party or vote contrary to party whips.

### 16 b) Explain various kinds of jurisdiction of Supreme Court. (8 marks)
The Supreme Court of India is one of the most powerful apex courts in the world, endowed with a broad and multifaceted jurisdiction by the Constitution to act as the ultimate interpreter of the law and the guarantor of fundamental rights. Its jurisdiction can be broadly classified into Original, Appellate, Advisory, and Writ jurisdictions.

**1. Original Jurisdiction (Article 131):** The Supreme Court has exclusive original jurisdiction to hear disputes of a federal nature. This means cases that arise directly between the Government of India and one or more States, or between two or more States, must be filed directly in the Supreme Court. This ensures the court acts as the federal umpire.

**2. Writ Jurisdiction (Article 32):** This is a critical component of its original jurisdiction, specifically for the enforcement of Fundamental Rights. Any citizen whose fundamental rights are violated can directly approach the Supreme Court, bypassing lower courts. The Court can issue writs like *Habeas Corpus*, *Mandamus*, *Prohibition*, *Certiorari*, and *Quo Warranto* to restore these rights.

**3. Appellate Jurisdiction:** The Supreme Court is the highest court of appeal in the country. It hears appeals against the judgments of High Courts in three main categories:
*   **Constitutional Matters (Article 132):** If a High Court certifies that a case involves a substantial question of law as to the interpretation of the Constitution.
*   **Civil Matters (Article 133):** If the High Court certifies that the case involves a substantial question of law of general importance that needs the Supreme Court's decision.
*   **Criminal Matters (Article 134):** An appeal lies if the High Court, on appeal, reversed an acquittal and sentenced the accused to death, or if it withdrew a case from a subordinate court and sentenced the accused to death.
Additionally, **Article 136** grants the Court discretionary power to grant Special Leave to Appeal against any judgment from any court or tribunal in India (except military tribunals).

**4. Advisory Jurisdiction (Article 143):** The President of India can seek the Supreme Court's opinion on any question of law or fact of public importance. While the Court's opinion is advisory and not binding on the President, it carries immense legal weight.

## Module - 4

### 17 a) Explain the powers and functions of the Governor of Kerala state. (8 marks)
The Governor is the constitutional head of the State of Kerala (as in other Indian states) and represents the President of India at the state level. While operating within a parliamentary framework where real executive power lies with the Chief Minister and the Council of Ministers, the Governor possesses significant executive, legislative, financial, and discretionary powers.

**1. Executive Powers:** All executive actions of the state government are taken in the name of the Governor. The Governor appoints the Chief Minister (usually the leader of the majority party in the Legislative Assembly) and other ministers on the Chief Minister's advice. The Governor also appoints the Advocate General of the State, the Chairman and members of the State Public Service Commission, and the State Election Commissioner. The Governor can seek information from the Chief Minister relating to the administration of the state.

**2. Legislative Powers:** The Governor is an integral part of the State Legislature. They have the power to summon, prorogue the State Legislative Assembly, and can dissolve it. The Governor addresses the first session of the legislature every year and the first session after a general election. Crucially, no bill passed by the state legislature becomes an Act without the Governor's assent. The Governor can give assent, withhold it, return the bill (if it's not a money bill) for reconsideration, or reserve it for the consideration of the President. The Governor can also promulgate Ordinances when the legislature is not in session.

**3. Financial Powers:** A Money Bill can only be introduced in the state legislature with the prior recommendation of the Governor. The Governor ensures that the Annual State Budget is laid before the legislature and constitutes the State Finance Commission every five years.

**4. Discretionary Powers:** Unlike the President, the Constitution explicitly grants some discretionary powers to the Governor. These are exercised without the advice of the Council of Ministers. They include recommending the imposition of President's Rule (Article 356) if the state's constitutional machinery breaks down, reserving certain bills for the President, and appointing a Chief Minister in the event of a hung assembly or sudden demise of a ruling CM without an obvious successor.

### 17 b) Explain the composition and duration of state legislative council. (6 marks)
The State Legislative Council, or Vidhan Parishad, is the upper house in states with a bicameral legislature. Unlike the Legislative Assembly, which is mandatory, the existence of the Legislative Council is optional. Article 169 empowers Parliament to create or abolish a Legislative Council in a state if the Legislative Assembly of that state passes a resolution to that effect by a special majority. 

**Composition:** The composition of the Legislative Council is designed to ensure representation from various sectors of society, making it a heterogeneous body. Article 171 dictates that the total number of members in a Legislative Council cannot exceed one-third of the total number of members in the state's Legislative Assembly, but it must not be less than 40 members. The election and nomination process is indirect and divided as follows:
*   1/3rd of the members are elected by local bodies like municipalities and district boards.
*   1/12th are elected by graduates of three years' standing residing in the state.
*   1/12th are elected by teachers of three years' standing in educational institutions not lower in standard than a secondary school.
*   1/3rd are elected by the members of the State Legislative Assembly from amongst persons who are not members of the Assembly.
*   The remainder (approximately 1/6th) are nominated by the Governor from individuals possessing special knowledge or practical experience in literature, science, art, cooperative movement, and social service.

**Duration:** The Legislative Council is a permanent body; it is not subject to dissolution. However, akin to the Rajya Sabha at the Centre, one-third of its members retire every second year. Consequently, a member of the Legislative Council enjoys a continuous term of six years.

### 18 a) Explain the qualification and disqualification for membership of the state legislature. (7 marks)
The State Legislature consists of the Governor and the Legislative Assembly (Vidhan Sabha), and in some states, a Legislative Council (Vidhan Parishad). The Constitution sets clear parameters for who can become a member of these legislative bodies.

**Qualifications:** Under Article 173, to be qualified for membership of the state legislature, a person must:
1. Be a citizen of India.
2. Make and subscribe to an oath or affirmation of allegiance to the Constitution before an authority authorized by the Election Commission.
3. Age criterion: Be at least 25 years old to contest for the Legislative Assembly, and at least 30 years old for the Legislative Council.
4. Possess any other qualifications prescribed by Parliament. For instance, the Representation of the People Act requires the candidate to be an elector for an assembly constituency in that state.

**Disqualifications:** Article 191 outlines the grounds for disqualification from being a member of the State Legislative Assembly or Legislative Council. A person is disqualified if they:
1. Hold any office of profit under the Government of India or the Government of any State, unless that office is exempted by a state law.
2. Are declared to be of unsound mind by a competent court.
3. Are an undischarged insolvent.
4. Are not a citizen of India, or have voluntarily acquired the citizenship of a foreign state, or owe allegiance to a foreign state.
5. Are disqualified under any law made by Parliament. This includes disqualifications for electoral offenses, corruption, or criminal convictions under the Representation of the People Act, 1951.
Furthermore, the Tenth Schedule of the Constitution mandates disqualification on grounds of defection—if a member voluntarily gives up the membership of their political party or disobeys party directives (whips) during voting in the house.

### 18 b) Explain the constitution of High court. What are the essential qualifications required for the appointment of High court Judge? (7 marks)
The High Court is the highest judicial authority in a state, playing a pivotal role in the integrated judicial system of India. Governed by Part VI, Chapter V of the Constitution, High Courts have wide-ranging jurisdiction, including original, appellate, and writ jurisdictions, enabling them to protect fundamental rights and exercise supervisory control over all subordinate courts and tribunals within their territorial limits.

**Constitution of the High Court:** Article 214 stipulates that there shall be a High Court for each state. However, Parliament has the power (under Article 231) to establish a common High Court for two or more states or for two or more states and a Union Territory. Structurally, a High Court consists of a Chief Justice and such other number of Judges as the President of India may deem necessary to appoint from time to time. Unlike the Supreme Court, the Constitution does not fix the maximum number of judges for a High Court; it varies depending on the volume of litigation and the size of the state.

**Qualifications for Appointment:** To ensure the judiciary's competence and independence, Article 217 lays down stringent qualifications for the appointment of a High Court judge. To be eligible, a person must:
1. Be a citizen of India.
2. Have held a judicial office in the territory of India for at least ten years.
   *OR*
3. Have been an advocate of a High Court (or of two or more such Courts in succession) for at least ten years. 
The judges of the High Court are appointed by the President. The Chief Justice of the High Court is appointed after consultation with the Chief Justice of India and the Governor of the state concerned. For appointing other judges, the Chief Justice of that specific High Court is also consulted. 

## Module - 5

### 19 a) Explain parliamentary legislation in the state field. (6 marks)
The Indian Constitution fundamentally divides legislative powers between the Centre and the States through the Seventh Schedule (Union List, State List, Concurrent List). Normally, Parliament legislates on Union and Concurrent List subjects, while State Legislatures have exclusive power over State List subjects. However, the Constitution allows for "Parliamentary legislation in the State field" under certain extraordinary circumstances to ensure national interest and uniformity. 

Parliament can make laws on subjects enumerated in the State List in five specific situations:
1. **National Interest (Article 249):** If the Rajya Sabha passes a resolution supported by a special majority (two-thirds of members present and voting) declaring that it is necessary in the national interest, Parliament gains the power to legislate on the specified State List subject for one year.
2. **National Emergency (Article 250):** During the operation of a National Emergency under Article 352, Parliament assumes the power to legislate on any subject in the State List for the entire country or part of it.
3. **State Consent (Article 252):** If the legislatures of two or more states pass resolutions requesting Parliament to enact a law on a State List subject, Parliament can do so. Such a law applies only to those consenting states (and any other state that adopts it later).
4. **International Agreements (Article 253):** Parliament has overriding power to make laws for the whole or any part of India to implement any treaty, agreement, or convention with foreign countries, even if the subject falls under the State List.
5. **President's Rule (Article 356/357):** When a state is under President's Rule due to the failure of constitutional machinery, Parliament is empowered to make laws for that state concerning any subject, including those in the State List.

### 19 b) Discuss the effects of national and financial emergencies. (8 marks)
Emergency provisions in the Indian Constitution are designed to convert the normal federal structure into a unitary one temporarily, enabling the central government to deal effectively with severe crises. The effects of National and Financial Emergencies are profound and impact executive, legislative, and financial relations.

**Effects of National Emergency (Article 352):** A National Emergency can be declared on grounds of war, external aggression, or armed rebellion. 
1. **Executive and Legislative:** The federal balance shifts dramatically. The Centre gains the power to give executive directions to states on any matter. Parliament acquires the power to legislate on any subject in the State List (Article 250). State governments are not suspended, but they come under the complete control of the Centre.
2. **Financial:** The President can modify the constitutional distribution of revenues between the Centre and the States, suspending or reducing the financial transfers to states to retain funds for national defense.
3. **Fundamental Rights:** Article 358 automatically suspends the six fundamental freedoms under Article 19 as soon as an emergency is declared on grounds of war or external aggression (not armed rebellion). Under Article 359, the President can issue an order suspending the right to move courts for the enforcement of other Fundamental Rights, except those under Articles 20 (protection in respect of conviction) and 21 (protection of life and personal liberty). 

**Effects of Financial Emergency (Article 360):** Declared when the financial stability or credit of India is threatened.
1. The executive authority of the Centre expands to give directions to states to observe financial propriety.
2. The Centre can order the reduction of salaries and allowances of all or any class of persons serving the State, and even those serving the Union, including Judges of the Supreme Court and High Courts.
3. The President can direct that all money bills or other financial bills passed by the state legislatures be reserved for their consideration. This severely curtails the financial autonomy of the states.

### 20 a) Explain the procedure for amendment of the constitution. (6 marks)
The procedure for amending the Constitution of India is laid down in Article 368. The framers intentionally designed a mechanism that is neither as easy as enacting ordinary laws (which would make the constitution too unstable) nor as difficult as the American amendment process (which would make it too rigid to adapt to changing times).

The procedure under Article 368 involves several steps:
1. **Initiation:** An amendment can be initiated only by the introduction of a bill for that purpose in either House of Parliament (Lok Sabha or Rajya Sabha). It cannot be initiated in the state legislatures. The bill can be introduced by a minister or a private member and does not require the prior permission of the President.
2. **Special Majority:** The bill must be passed in each House by a "special majority," which means a majority of the total membership of that House AND a majority of not less than two-thirds of the members of that House present and voting.
3. **State Ratification (Conditional):** If the amendment seeks to alter the federal provisions of the Constitution (such as the election of the President, distribution of legislative powers, or representation of states in Parliament), the bill must also be ratified by the legislatures of at least one-half of the states by a simple majority before it is presented to the President.
4. **Presidential Assent:** After being passed by both Houses (and ratified by states, if necessary), the bill is presented to the President. Following the 24th Amendment Act, the President is bound to give their assent to a constitutional amendment bill; they cannot withhold assent or return it for reconsideration. Upon receiving the assent, the Constitution stands amended.

### 20 b) What is the need for administrative tribunals? Explain the functions of state administrative tribunals. (8 marks)
Administrative tribunals are quasi-judicial bodies established by the government to resolve disputes related to administrative matters, taxation, and service conditions of public servants. The growing complexity of modern governance, the expansion of state functions into socio-economic spheres, and the resulting increase in litigation led to a massive backlog of cases in traditional courts. Ordinary courts follow strict, rigid procedural laws (like the Civil Procedure Code) which are time-consuming and expensive. Therefore, administrative tribunals were needed to provide a mechanism that is speedy, inexpensive, and accessible. Furthermore, technical disputes (like environmental issues or taxation) require specialized knowledge that ordinary judges might lack; tribunals are often staffed with both legal experts and subject-matter specialists, ensuring more informed adjudication.

**State Administrative Tribunals (SATs):** Recognizing the need to address grievances of state government employees efficiently, the 42nd Amendment Act of 1976 introduced Part XIV-A to the Constitution. Article 323A empowered Parliament to establish administrative tribunals for the Union and the States. Subsequently, Parliament enacted the Administrative Tribunals Act, 1985, which authorized the Central Government to establish SATs upon the specific request of the concerned State Government.

**Functions of SATs:**
1. **Adjudication of Service Disputes:** The primary function of a SAT is to adjudicate disputes and complaints regarding the recruitment and conditions of service of persons appointed to public services and posts in connection with the affairs of the state. This includes matters like promotions, transfers, disciplinary proceedings, pensions, and dismissals.
2. **Alternative Forum:** Once a SAT is established, it assumes original jurisdiction over all service matters of state employees, effectively barring the jurisdiction of all other courts (except the Supreme Court under Article 136, and later High Courts under Articles 226/227 as ruled in the *L. Chandra Kumar* case). 
3. **Procedural Flexibility:** SATs are not bound by the strict rules of the Code of Civil Procedure or the Indian Evidence Act. They are guided by the principles of natural justice, which allows them to adopt flexible, rapid procedures, drastically reducing the time taken to resolve disputes compared to High Courts.


## Constitution of India (July 2021) Answer Key_Part1.md

# Constitution of India (July 2021) Answer Key - Part 1

## PART A (Expanded Answers for 3-mark questions)

**1. Define Constitution. Why is it necessary for a country?**
A constitution can be comprehensively defined as the fundamental law of a nation or state, establishing the character and conception of its government, laying the basic principles to which its internal life is to be conformed, organizing the government, and regulating, distributing, and limiting the functions of its different departments. It prescribes the extent and manner of the exercise of sovereign powers. A constitution is not just a legal document; it is a political and social charter that reflects the aspirations and values of a society. It is the supreme law of the land, meaning any other law or action by the government must align with its principles.
The necessity of a constitution for a country is manifold. Firstly, it provides a set of basic rules that allow for minimal coordination amongst members of a society. This coordination is crucial for maintaining peace and order in any complex, diverse society. Secondly, it specifies who has the power to make decisions in a society. It establishes the framework of the government, detailing the powers and responsibilities of the legislative, executive, and judicial branches. This prevents the arbitrary exercise of power and ensures a system of checks and balances. Thirdly, it sets limits on what a government can impose on its citizens, primarily through the guarantee of fundamental rights. These rights protect the individual from the potential tyranny of the state. Lastly, a constitution enables the government to fulfill the aspirations of a society and create conditions for a just society, acting as an instrument of social change and progress.

**2. Differentiate between termination and deprivation of Indian citizenship.**
Termination and deprivation are two distinct modes by which an individual can lose their Indian citizenship under the Citizenship Act of 1955. 
Termination of citizenship occurs when an Indian citizen voluntarily and consciously acquires the citizenship of another country. The moment a person acquires the citizenship of another sovereign state, their Indian citizenship automatically ceases to exist. This is based on the principle that India does not allow dual citizenship for its nationals. The process is self-executing and happens by the operation of law, provided the person is of full age and capacity. However, there is an exception during times of war: if India is engaged in a war, the termination of citizenship is held in abeyance and does not happen automatically unless the Central Government agrees to it.
Deprivation of citizenship, on the other hand, is a compulsory termination of Indian citizenship by the Central Government. It is not voluntary but is imposed by the state under specific circumstances outlined in the Citizenship Act. Deprivation applies only to those who have acquired Indian citizenship by naturalization or registration. A person can be deprived of their citizenship if it was obtained by fraud, false representation, or the concealment of any material fact. It can also occur if the citizen has shown disloyalty to the Constitution of India, unlawfully traded or communicated with an enemy during a war, been imprisoned in any country for two years within five years of registration or naturalization, or has been ordinarily resident out of India for seven continuous years. Deprivation requires an executive order after giving the person a reasonable opportunity to be heard.

**3. How is State defined under Article 12 of Indian Constitution?**
Article 12 of the Indian Constitution provides an inclusive and expansive definition of the term "State" for the purpose of Part III (Fundamental Rights) and Part IV (Directive Principles of State Policy). According to Article 12, the "State" includes the Government and Parliament of India, the Government and the Legislature of each of the States, and all local or other authorities within the territory of India or under the control of the Government of India. 
The inclusion of the "Government and Parliament of India" covers the executive and legislative organs of the Union government. Similarly, "Government and the Legislature of each of the States" covers the executive and legislative organs of the state governments. "Local authorities" include municipalities, panchayats, improvement trusts, and other similar bodies that have the power to make rules and regulations and have autonomous status.
The most widely interpreted part of this definition is "other authorities." Through various judicial pronouncements, the Supreme Court of India has expanded this term to include bodies created by a statute, bodies exercising sovereign functions, and even private bodies or corporations that are financially, functionally, or administratively dominated by or under the pervasive control of the government. This expansion ensures that entities acting as instrumentalities or agencies of the state cannot violate the fundamental rights of citizens, thus providing broader protection to the individuals against arbitrary actions not just by the core government but also by bodies functioning on its behalf.

**4. What is the basic difference between Fundamental Rights and Directive Principles of State Policy?**
The Fundamental Rights (FRs) enshrined in Part III and the Directive Principles of State Policy (DPSPs) contained in Part IV of the Indian Constitution form the core of the constitutional philosophy, yet they differ significantly in their nature, enforceability, and objectives.
The primary difference lies in their justiciability. Fundamental Rights are justiciable in nature, meaning they are legally enforceable by the courts. If a citizen's fundamental right is violated, they can directly approach the High Courts or the Supreme Court for its enforcement. In contrast, Directive Principles are non-justiciable. They are not enforceable by any court of law. A citizen cannot sue the state for not implementing a directive principle.
Secondly, they differ in their overarching objectives. Fundamental Rights are primarily aimed at establishing political democracy. They act as negative obligations on the state, preventing it from doing certain things that would infringe upon individual liberties. They protect individuals against arbitrary state action. Directive Principles, however, are aimed at establishing social and economic democracy. They act as positive obligations on the state, directing it to achieve certain social and economic goals, such as equitable distribution of wealth and provision of adequate livelihood. While Fundamental Rights focus on the welfare of the individual, Directive Principles focus on the welfare of the community as a whole. Despite these differences, they are considered complementary, forming an integrated scheme for the realization of the goals set out in the Preamble.

**5. Explain the procedure for impeachment of the President.**
The President of India can be removed from office before the expiry of their term through a process called impeachment, exclusively on the ground of "violation of the Constitution." The procedure for impeachment is detailed in Article 61 of the Constitution and is a quasi-judicial process.
The impeachment process can be initiated by either house of the Parliament (Lok Sabha or Rajya Sabha). The charges must be framed in the form of a resolution. For the resolution to be introduced, it must be signed by at least one-fourth of the total members of the house that is framing the charges. Furthermore, a 14-day advance written notice must be given to the President regarding the intention to move such a resolution.
After the notice period, the resolution must be passed by a special majority of not less than two-thirds of the total membership of that house. Once passed by the initiating house, the charges are sent to the other house, which then investigates the charges or appoints a committee to do so. The President has the right to appear and be represented at such an investigation to defend themselves. If, after the investigation, the second house also passes a resolution sustaining the charges by a majority of not less than two-thirds of its total membership, the President stands removed from their office from the date on which such a resolution is passed. This stringent procedure ensures that the highest office is not subjected to frivolous removals.

**6. Mention the powers and Functions of Attorney General of India.**
The Attorney General of India is the highest law officer in the country, appointed by the President under Article 76 of the Constitution. The primary function of the Attorney General is to give advice to the Government of India upon such legal matters, and to perform such other duties of a legal character, as may from time to time be referred or assigned to them by the President.
Key functions include representing the Government of India in the Supreme Court in all cases (including suits, appeals, and other proceedings) in which the Government is concerned. They also represent the Government of India in any reference made by the President to the Supreme Court under Article 143 of the Constitution. Furthermore, the Attorney General has to appear in any High Court in any case in which the Government of India is concerned, if the Government requires them to do so.
In terms of powers, the Attorney General has the right of audience in all courts within the territory of India. Uniquely, the Attorney General has the right to speak in, and otherwise to take part in the proceedings of, either House of Parliament, any joint sitting of the Houses, and any committee of Parliament of which they may be named a member. However, they do not have the right to vote in these proceedings. They enjoy all the privileges and immunities that are available to a Member of Parliament. While acting as the top legal advisor, they are not a full-time counsel for the Government and are not debarred from private legal practice, provided it does not conflict with their duties to the state.

**7. Why do we need to form separate Union Territories?**
Union Territories (UTs) are federal territories in India that are administered directly by the Central Government, unlike the states which have their own elected governments. The formation of separate Union Territories is necessitated by various geographical, cultural, administrative, and political reasons.
Firstly, some territories are geographically isolated or too small to be formed into separate independent states. Examples include the Andaman and Nicobar Islands and Lakshadweep. Their remote location and strategic importance in the Indian Ocean make direct central control vital for national security and effective administration. Secondly, cultural distinctiveness is a significant factor. Territories like Puducherry, Daman and Diu, and Dadra and Nagar Haveli have unique cultural heritages influenced by former French or Portuguese colonial rule. Maintaining them as UTs helps in preserving their distinct cultural identity while integrating them into the Indian Union.
Thirdly, political and administrative considerations often demand direct central control. Delhi, being the National Capital, requires special administrative attention and direct central oversight to ensure the smooth functioning of the national government institutions and diplomatic missions located there. Similarly, Chandigarh serves as the joint capital of Punjab and Haryana, and making it a UT was an administrative necessity to resolve the conflict between the two states over its control. Recently, the reorganization of Jammu and Kashmir into two UTs (J&K and Ladakh) was driven by complex political and security considerations. In essence, UTs are formed when an area cannot be merged with neighboring states and is not large or viable enough to become a state of its own, requiring the direct stewardship of the Union government.

**8. Distinguish between an 'Ordinary Bill' and 'Money Bill'.**
In the Indian parliamentary system, legislative proposals are brought before the Parliament in the form of bills. The two most common types are Ordinary Bills and Money Bills, which differ significantly in their subject matter, introduction process, and the powers of the two houses.
A Money Bill is strictly defined under Article 110 of the Constitution. It contains provisions dealing exclusively with matters such as taxation (imposition, abolition, remission, alteration), borrowing of money by the government, the custody of the Consolidated Fund or Contingency Fund of India, or the appropriation of money out of the Consolidated Fund. An Ordinary Bill, on the other hand, deals with any general matter of public importance other than financial subjects and constitutional amendments. 
The procedure for their passage varies considerably. A Money Bill can only be introduced in the Lok Sabha (the lower house) and only on the recommendation of the President. An Ordinary Bill can be introduced in either the Lok Sabha or the Rajya Sabha (the upper house) and does not require the President's prior recommendation. Once passed by the Lok Sabha, the Rajya Sabha has very limited powers regarding a Money Bill. It can only make recommendations, which the Lok Sabha may or may not accept, and it must return the bill within 14 days. If it fails to do so, the bill is deemed passed. For an Ordinary Bill, both houses have almost equal powers. If there is a deadlock over an Ordinary Bill, the President can summon a joint sitting of both houses to resolve it. No joint sitting can be summoned for a Money Bill. Furthermore, the President cannot return a Money Bill for reconsideration, whereas they can do so for an Ordinary Bill.

**9. What are the constitutional provisions relating to freedom of trade, commerce and intercourse?**
The constitutional provisions relating to the freedom of trade, commerce, and intercourse within the territory of India are primarily enshrined in Part XIII of the Constitution, covering Articles 301 to 307. The underlying objective of these provisions is to break down the border barriers between states and create a single economic unit, fostering a sense of national unity and ensuring the unhindered flow of goods and services.
Article 301 establishes the general principle that trade, commerce, and intercourse throughout the territory of India shall be free. This freedom is not just from internal customs duties but also from any other restrictions that may impede the free movement of goods, thereby aiming to establish India as one unified common market. 
However, this freedom is not absolute and is subject to certain restrictions outlined in subsequent articles. Article 302 empowers the Parliament to impose restrictions on this freedom in the "public interest." For instance, essential commodities can be regulated during shortages. Article 303 prohibits both Parliament and State Legislatures from making laws that discriminate between one state and another, except when Parliament acts to deal with a situation of scarcity of goods in any part of India. Article 304 empowers State Legislatures to impose reasonable restrictions on the freedom of trade, commerce, or intercourse with or within that state in the public interest, provided that such a bill requires the prior sanction of the President. It also allows states to impose non-discriminatory taxes on goods imported from other states. Lastly, Article 307 provides for the appointment of an authority by Parliament to carry out the purposes of these articles, though no such authority has been constituted to date.

**10. List out the three types of emergencies under Indian Constitution.**
The Constitution of India envisions extraordinary situations where the normal functioning of the democratic and federal structure needs to be suspended to protect the nation's integrity, security, and stability. To handle such unforeseen crises, Part XVIII of the Constitution provides for three specific types of emergencies.
The first type is the National Emergency, provided under Article 352. The President can declare a National Emergency if the security of India or a part of it is threatened by war, external aggression, or armed rebellion. This declaration shifts the federal structure into a unitary one, giving the Union government sweeping powers over the states, and it can also lead to the suspension of certain Fundamental Rights. It requires parliamentary approval within a month and can be extended indefinitely, subject to periodic approvals.
The second type is the State Emergency, commonly known as President's Rule, provided under Article 356. This is declared when there is a breakdown of constitutional machinery in a specific state. If the President is satisfied, either on the report of the state's Governor or otherwise, that the government of the state cannot be carried on in accordance with the provisions of the Constitution, they can dismiss the state government and assume the executive powers of the state, while the state legislature is suspended or dissolved.
The third type is the Financial Emergency, provided under Article 360. If the President is satisfied that a situation has arisen whereby the financial stability or credit of India or any part of its territory is threatened, they can declare a Financial Emergency. During this period, the executive authority of the Union extends to giving directions to any state to observe specified canons of financial propriety, and it allows for the reduction of salaries and allowances of all or any class of persons serving the State or the Union, including judges of the Supreme Court and High Courts.


## Constitution of India (July 2021) Answer Key_Part2.md

# Constitution of India (July 2021) Answer Key - Part 2

## PART B (Expanded Answers for 14-mark questions)

**11. a) What is Preamble? Can it be used for the interpretation of the constitution? Also explain its significance. (8)**
**11. b) Describe the salient features of Indian Constitution. (6)**

The Preamble to the Indian Constitution serves as a brief introductory statement that sets out the guiding purpose, principles, and philosophy of the document. It is often referred to as the 'soul of the Constitution' or its 'identity card.' The Preamble is based on the 'Objectives Resolution' drafted and moved by Jawaharlal Nehru, and adopted by the Constituent Assembly. It declares India to be a Sovereign, Socialist, Secular, Democratic, and Republic state. It outlines the primary objectives to secure for all citizens: Justice (social, economic, and political), Liberty (of thought, expression, belief, faith, and worship), Equality (of status and of opportunity), and to promote Fraternity, assuring the dignity of the individual and the unity and integrity of the nation.
Regarding its role in the interpretation of the Constitution, the Supreme Court's stance has evolved. Initially, in the Berubari Union case (1960), the Court held that the Preamble is not a part of the Constitution and therefore cannot be used to interpret ambiguous provisions. However, this view was decisively overturned in the landmark Kesavananda Bharati case (1973). Here, the Supreme Court recognized that the Preamble is indeed an integral part of the Constitution. The Court established that while the Preamble itself does not grant power or impose limitations, it is of extreme importance for interpreting the Constitution. Whenever there is ambiguity in the text of the Constitution, the courts rely on the Preamble to ascertain the true meaning and intent of the framers. It acts as a guiding light for judicial interpretation. The significance of the Preamble lies in its embodiment of the fundamental values and the philosophical vision of the Indian state. It serves as a yardstick by which the performance of the government can be measured.
The Indian Constitution, adopted in 1949 and enacted in 1950, possesses several salient features that distinguish it from other global constitutions. Firstly, it is the lengthiest written constitution in the world, comprehensive and detailed, reflecting the country's vast diversity and administrative complexities. Secondly, it exhibits a unique blend of rigidity and flexibility; some provisions require a special majority for amendment, while others can be amended by a simple parliamentary majority. Thirdly, it establishes a Parliamentary form of government, characterized by the presence of nominal and real executives, majority party rule, and collective responsibility of the executive to the legislature. Fourthly, it features a federal system with unitary bias; while it divides power between the center and states, the center remains exceptionally strong, especially during emergencies. Fifthly, the Constitution guarantees Fundamental Rights to all citizens, safeguarding individual liberties against state encroachment, while simultaneously prescribing Fundamental Duties. Furthermore, it incorporates Directive Principles of State Policy, serving as a moral compass for the government to establish a welfare state. Other notable features include an independent and integrated judiciary, universal adult franchise, single citizenship, and the provision for emergency powers.

**12. a) Give detail account on the historical background of Indian Constitution. (6)**
**12. b) What is citizenship? Discuss the various methods of acquiring Indian citizenship. (8)**

The historical background of the Indian Constitution is deeply rooted in the various acts passed by the British Parliament during their rule in India. The journey towards a constitutional framework began with the Regulating Act of 1773, which initiated the centralization of administration under the East India Company. Subsequent acts, such as the Pitt's India Act of 1784 and the Charter Acts of 1833 and 1853, further refined the administrative machinery and introduced nascent forms of legislative processes. The watershed moment came after the Revolt of 1857, leading to the Government of India Act 1858, which transferred power directly to the British Crown. The Indian Councils Acts of 1861, 1892, and 1909 gradually increased Indian representation in legislative councils, though on a limited scale. The Government of India Act 1919 introduced 'dyarchy' in the provinces, dividing subjects between the executive and the legislature. However, the most significant precursor to the current Constitution was the Government of India Act 1935. It proposed an All India Federation, introduced provincial autonomy, and established a Federal Court. The drafting of the Constitution itself was the monumental task of the Constituent Assembly, formed in 1946 under the Cabinet Mission Plan. The Assembly, comprising diverse representatives, debated intensely for nearly three years, drawing inspiration from various global constitutions and the 1935 Act, culminating in the adoption of the Constitution on November 26, 1949.
Citizenship, in legal terms, denotes the formal relationship between an individual and a state, granting the individual certain rights and imposing specific duties. It represents full and equal membership in a political community. In India, citizenship is primarily governed by Part II of the Constitution (Articles 5-11) and the Citizenship Act of 1955. India follows the principle of single citizenship, meaning individuals are citizens of India, not of their respective states. The Citizenship Act, 1955, amended several times, prescribes five principal methods for acquiring Indian citizenship. 
1. By Birth: A person born in India on or after specific dates (with conditions regarding the parents' citizenship status) automatically becomes a citizen.
2. By Descent: A person born outside India on or after specific dates can acquire citizenship if one or both parents are Indian citizens, subject to registration requirements.
3. By Registration: Individuals of Indian origin ordinarily resident in India for a specified period, or those married to Indian citizens, can apply for citizenship through registration.
4. By Naturalization: A foreigner can acquire Indian citizenship by naturalization if they have resided in India for a prolonged period (usually 11 of the previous 14 years), renounce their previous citizenship, are of good character, and possess adequate knowledge of a recognized Indian language.
5. By Incorporation of Territory: If a new territory becomes a part of India, the Government of India can specify the persons from that territory who shall become citizens of India.

**13. a) Describe the Right to Constitutional Remedies and explain its significance. (6)**
**13. b) Explain the needs and importance of fundamental duties of an Indian Citizen. (8)**

The Right to Constitutional Remedies, enshrined in Article 32 of the Indian Constitution, is described by Dr. B.R. Ambedkar as the "heart and soul" of the Constitution. This right guarantees individuals the ability to approach the Supreme Court directly for the enforcement of their Fundamental Rights. Without this provision, the Fundamental Rights would be mere declarations without any practical utility. Article 32 empowers the Supreme Court to issue directions, orders, or writs—specifically Habeas Corpus, Mandamus, Prohibition, Quo Warranto, and Certiorari—for the enforcement of any right conferred by Part III. This right itself is a Fundamental Right, meaning it cannot be suspended except as provided by the Constitution (such as during a National Emergency). The significance of Article 32 cannot be overstated. It acts as the ultimate guarantor of individual liberties, providing a swift and potent mechanism against state excesses or arbitrary actions. It ensures that the state apparatus respects the constitutional boundaries and protects the citizens from systemic abuses of power. By making the enforcement of rights a fundamental right itself, the framers of the Constitution ensured that democracy in India remains substantive and not just procedural.
Fundamental Duties were not part of the original Constitution but were added by the 42nd Amendment Act in 1976 upon the recommendation of the Swaran Singh Committee. Enshrined in Article 51A, these duties outline the moral obligations of all citizens to help promote a spirit of patriotism and uphold the unity of India. The need for these duties arises from the principle that rights and duties are correlative and inseparable. A society where citizens only demand rights without recognizing their responsibilities towards the state and fellow citizens is inherently unstable. The duties serve several crucial purposes. Firstly, they act as a constant reminder to citizens that while enjoying their rights, they must also be conscious of their obligations to their country, society, and fellow beings. Secondly, they serve as a warning against anti-national and anti-social activities, such as burning the national flag, destroying public property, or promoting communal discord. Thirdly, they promote a sense of discipline and commitment among the citizenry, fostering active participation in the realization of national goals. Lastly, they help the courts in examining and determining the constitutional validity of a law. If a law seeks to give effect to any fundamental duty, the courts may consider it reasonable in relation to Article 14 or 19. While non-justiciable, they establish a normative framework for civic behavior.

**14. a) What are Fundamental Rights? Examine each of them. (8)**
**14. b) State the Directive Principles of State Policy and explain its significance. (6)**

Fundamental Rights, contained in Part III (Articles 12-35) of the Indian Constitution, are the basic human rights guaranteed to all citizens. They are 'fundamental' because they are essential for the holistic development (material, intellectual, moral, and spiritual) of individuals. They are justiciable, meaning individuals can move the courts for their enforcement if violated. The Constitution guarantees six broad categories of Fundamental Rights:
1. Right to Equality (Articles 14-18): Ensures equality before the law, prohibits discrimination on grounds of religion, race, caste, sex, or place of birth, guarantees equality of opportunity in public employment, and abolishes untouchability and titles.
2. Right to Freedom (Articles 19-22): Guarantees six essential freedoms: speech and expression, assembly, association, movement, residence, and profession. It also provides protection in respect of conviction for offenses, protection of life and personal liberty (Article 21), and protection against arbitrary arrest and detention.
3. Right against Exploitation (Articles 23-24): Prohibits human trafficking, forced labor (begar), and the employment of children under 14 years in hazardous jobs.
4. Right to Freedom of Religion (Articles 25-28): Guarantees freedom of conscience and the right to freely profess, practice, and propagate any religion. It also provides the freedom to manage religious affairs and freedom from paying taxes for the promotion of any particular religion.
5. Cultural and Educational Rights (Articles 29-30): Protects the interests of minorities by allowing them to conserve their distinct language, script, or culture, and grants them the right to establish and administer educational institutions of their choice.
6. Right to Constitutional Remedies (Article 32): Empowers citizens to move the Supreme Court for the enforcement of the aforementioned rights through the issuance of writs.
The Directive Principles of State Policy (DPSP), found in Part IV (Articles 36-51), are guidelines or principles given to the central and state governments to keep in mind while framing laws and policies. Though non-justiciable, they are fundamental in the governance of the country. Their significance lies in their aim to establish a welfare state, focusing on social and economic democracy, as opposed to the political democracy aimed at by Fundamental Rights. The DPSPs direct the state to ensure equitable distribution of material resources, prevent the concentration of wealth, secure a living wage, provide public assistance in cases of unemployment or old age, and organize agriculture on modern lines. They embody the aspirations of the people and provide a benchmark to evaluate the government's performance. By constantly striving to implement these principles, the state endeavors to create a society based on social justice and economic equality.

**15. a) Explain the various jurisdictions of the Supreme Court of India. (7)**
**15. b) Describe the constitutional duties and powers of the Prime Minister. (7)**

The Supreme Court of India stands at the apex of the judicial system and possesses extensive jurisdictions, making it one of the most powerful courts in the world. Its jurisdictions can be broadly classified into:
1. Original Jurisdiction (Article 131): The Supreme Court functions as a federal court. It has exclusive original jurisdiction to hear disputes between the Government of India and one or more States, or between two or more States. This ensures the resolution of federal conflicts. Furthermore, under Article 32, it has original jurisdiction regarding the enforcement of Fundamental Rights.
2. Appellate Jurisdiction: The Supreme Court is the highest court of appeal. Its appellate jurisdiction extends to constitutional, civil, and criminal matters. It hears appeals against the judgments of High Courts if a substantial question of law regarding constitutional interpretation is involved. In civil cases, it hears appeals if the High Court certifies that a substantial question of law of general importance is involved. In criminal cases, appeals lie if the High Court has, on appeal, reversed an order of acquittal and sentenced the accused to death.
3. Advisory Jurisdiction (Article 143): The President can refer to the Supreme Court any question of law or fact of public importance for its opinion. While the Court's advice is not binding on the President, it carries immense legal weight and helps the executive navigate complex constitutional issues.
4. Writ Jurisdiction (Article 32): It has the power to issue prerogative writs (Habeas Corpus, Mandamus, Prohibition, Quo Warranto, and Certiorari) for the enforcement of Fundamental Rights.
5. Review Jurisdiction (Article 137): The Court has the power to review any judgment pronounced or order made by it, ensuring self-correction and the rectification of manifest errors.
The Prime Minister of India is the real executive head and the head of the government. The constitutional duties and powers of the Prime Minister are vast. Firstly, concerning the Council of Ministers, the Prime Minister recommends persons to the President for appointment as ministers, allocates and reshuffles their portfolios, and presides over cabinet meetings, thus steering the policies of the government. The Prime Minister's resignation or death automatically dissolves the Council of Ministers. Secondly, concerning the President, the Prime Minister is the principal channel of communication between the President and the Council of Ministers. It is the Prime Minister's duty to communicate all decisions of the Council relating to administration and proposed legislation to the President. They advise the President on crucial appointments like the Attorney General, CAG, and Election Commissioners. Thirdly, in relation to Parliament, the Prime Minister advises the President on summoning and proroguing parliamentary sessions and can recommend the dissolution of the Lok Sabha at any time. As the chief spokesperson of the government on the floor of the House, the Prime Minister announces major policies. Thus, the Prime Minister holds the most pivotal position in the Indian politico-administrative system.

**16. a) Discuss the functions and powers of the President of India. (8)**
**16. b) Explain in detail about the Union Government structure and functions. (6)**

The President of India is the head of the state, the first citizen of India, and the symbol of national unity. While the President is a nominal executive (de jure head), the office carries significant constitutional powers and functions.
1. Executive Powers: All executive actions of the Government of India are formally taken in the President's name. The President appoints the Prime Minister and, on their advice, other ministers. The President appoints key constitutional functionaries including the Attorney General, the Comptroller and Auditor General, the Chief Election Commissioner, the Chairman of the UPSC, and Governors of states. The President also administers Union Territories directly through appointed administrators.
2. Legislative Powers: The President is an integral part of the Parliament. The President summons and prorogues the Parliament and addresses it at the commencement of the first session after each general election. A bill passed by Parliament becomes an act only after receiving the President's assent. The President has veto powers (Absolute, Suspensive, and Pocket) over bills. Crucially, when Parliament is not in session, the President can promulgate ordinances (Article 123) having the same force as parliamentary acts.
3. Judicial Powers: The President appoints the Chief Justice and other judges of the Supreme Court and High Courts. The President possesses the pardoning power (Article 72), allowing them to grant pardons, reprieves, respites, or remissions of punishment, or to suspend, remit, or commute the sentence of any person convicted of an offense.
4. Emergency Powers: The President has extraordinary powers to declare National Emergency (Article 352), State Emergency/President's Rule (Article 356), and Financial Emergency (Article 360).
5. Military and Diplomatic Powers: The President is the Supreme Commander of the defense forces. Diplomatic relations, treaties, and international agreements are negotiated and concluded in the President's name.
The Union Government structure in India follows a parliamentary system based on the Westminster model. It comprises three interdependent branches: the Executive, the Legislature, and the Judiciary. The Executive consists of the President, the Vice-President, and the Council of Ministers headed by the Prime Minister. Its primary function is the implementation of laws and the day-to-day administration of the country. The real executive power vests in the Council of Ministers, which is collectively responsible to the Lok Sabha. The Legislature is a bicameral Parliament consisting of the President, the Rajya Sabha (Council of States), and the Lok Sabha (House of the People). Its main function is law-making, holding the executive accountable, and controlling the national finances. The Judiciary is an integrated system headed by the Supreme Court, independent of the executive and legislature. Its function is to interpret laws, protect the Constitution, and adjudicate disputes, ensuring the rule of law. This structure ensures a system of checks and balances, vital for the functioning of a democratic republic.

**17. a) Explain the various writs issued by High court of Kerala. (6)**
**17. b) Discuss the constitutional position and powers of Governor. (8)**

The High Court of Kerala, like any other High Court in India, is empowered under Article 226 of the Constitution to issue various prerogative writs for the enforcement of Fundamental Rights and for any other purpose. These writs act as powerful constitutional remedies.
1. Habeas Corpus: Meaning "to have the body of," this writ is a bulwark of individual liberty against arbitrary detention. It directs the detaining authority to produce the detained person before the court to examine the legality of the detention. If the detention is found illegal, the court orders the person's immediate release.
2. Mandamus: Meaning "we command," this writ is issued to a public official, public body, corporation, or inferior court directing them to perform their mandatory legal duties, which they have failed or refused to perform. It ensures public authorities do not abdicate their responsibilities.
3. Prohibition: This writ is issued by a higher court to a lower court or tribunal to prevent it from exceeding its jurisdiction or usurping a jurisdiction with which it is not legally vested. It acts primarily in a preventive capacity.
4. Certiorari: Similar to prohibition, but issued to quash the order or decision of a lower court or tribunal after it has been made, usually on grounds of excess of jurisdiction, lack of jurisdiction, or an error of law apparent on the face of the record. It acts in both preventive and curative capacities.
5. Quo Warranto: Meaning "by what authority," this writ is issued to inquire into the legality of a person's claim to a public office. It prevents the illegal usurpation of public offices by individuals.
The Governor is the constitutional head of a state, functioning similarly to the President at the Union level. Appointed by the President, the Governor holds office during the President's pleasure. The Governor acts as a vital link between the Union and the State. While primarily a nominal head, the Governor possesses significant executive, legislative, and discretionary powers. All executive actions of the state government are taken in the Governor's name. The Governor appoints the Chief Minister and other ministers, the Advocate General, and the members of the State Public Service Commission. Legislatively, the Governor summons and prorogues the state legislature and gives assent to bills passed by it. The Governor can withhold assent, return ordinary bills for reconsideration, or reserve certain bills for the President's consideration.
